Not to be confused with [Fourier method][1] or Fourier's original [sine and cosine transforms][2].

In [mathematics][3], the **Fourier transform** (**FT**) is an [integral transform][4] that takes a
[function][5] as input and outputs another function that describes the extent to which various
[frequencies][6] are present in the original function. The output of the transform is a [complex
valued function][7] of frequency. The term Fourier transform refers to both the [mathematical
operation][8] and to this complex-valued function. When a distinction needs to be made, the output
of the operation is sometimes called the [frequency domain][9] representation of the original
function. The Fourier transform is analogous to decomposing the [sound][10] of a musical [chord][11]
into the [intensities][12] of its constituent [pitches][13].

Functions that are localized in the [time domain][14] have Fourier transforms that are spread out
across the frequency domain and vice versa, a phenomenon known as the [uncertainty principle][15].
The [critical][16] case for this principle is the [Gaussian function][17], of substantial importance
in [probability theory][18] and [statistics][19] as well as in the study of physical phenomena
exhibiting [normal distribution][20] (e.g., [diffusion][21]). The Fourier transform of a Gaussian
function is another Gaussian function. [Joseph Fourier][22] introduced [sine and cosine
transforms][23] (which [correspond to the imaginary and real components][24] of the modern Fourier
transform) in his study of [heat transfer][25], where Gaussian functions appear as solutions of the
[heat equation][26].

The Fourier transform can be formally defined as an [improper][27] [Riemann integral][28], making it
an integral transform, although this definition is not suitable for many applications requiring a
more sophisticated integration theory.^{[[note 2]][29]} For example, many relatively simple
applications use the [Dirac delta function][30], which can be treated formally as if it were a
function, but the justification requires a mathematically more sophisticated viewpoint.^{[[note
3]][31]}

The Fourier transform can also be generalized to functions of several variables on [Euclidean
space][32], sending a function of 3-dimensional "position space" to a function of 3-dimensional
momentum (or a function of space and time to a function of [4-momentum][33]). This idea makes the
spatial Fourier transform very natural in the study of waves, as well as in [quantum mechanics][34],
where it is important to be able to represent wave solutions as functions of either position or
momentum and sometimes both. In general, functions to which Fourier methods are applicable are
complex-valued, and possibly [vector-valued][35].^{[[note 4]][36]} Still further generalization is
possible to functions on [groups][37], which, besides the original Fourier transform on [**R**][38]
or **R**^{n}, notably includes the [discrete-time Fourier transform][39] (DTFT, group =
[**Z**][40]), the [discrete Fourier transform][41] (DFT, group = [**Z** mod N][42]) and the [Fourier
series][43] or circular Fourier transform (group = [S¹][44], the unit circle ≈ closed finite
interval with endpoints identified). The latter is routinely employed to handle [periodic
functions][45]. The [fast Fourier transform][46] (FFT) is an algorithm for computing the DFT.

## Definition

The Fourier transform of a [Lebesgue integrable][47] complex-valued function f ( x ) {\displaystyle
f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}] on the real line, is the complex valued function ⁠f ^ ( ξ )
{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi )} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi )}]⁠, defined by the
integral^{[[1]][48]}

Fourier transform

───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬───
f ^ ( ξ ) = ∫ − ∞ ∞ f ( x )   e − i 2 π ξ x d x , ∀ ξ ∈ R . {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi  │Eq.
)=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }f(x)\ e^{-i2\pi \xi x}\,dx,\quad \forall \xi \in \mathbb {R} .}    │1  
[{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi )=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }f(x)\ e^{-i2\pi \xi x}\,dx,\quad│   
\forall \xi \in \mathbb {R} .}]                                                                │   
───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴───

When f ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}] is (Lebesgue) integrable over the whole
real line, the above integral converges for all ξ ∈ R {\displaystyle \xi \in \mathbb {R} }
[{\displaystyle \xi \in \mathbb {R} }], and f ^ ( ξ ) {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi )}
[{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi )}] is a [uniformly continuous][49] function of ξ {\displaystyle
\xi } [{\displaystyle \xi }] which decays to zero as ⁠ξ → ∞ {\displaystyle \xi \to \infty }
[{\displaystyle \xi \to \infty }]⁠.

However, the Fourier transform can also be defined for (generalized) functions for which the
[Lebesgue integral][50] **[Eq.1][51]** does not make sense.^{[[2]][52]} Interpreting the integral
[suitably][53] (e.g. as an [improper integral][54] for [locally integrable][55] functions) extends
the Fourier transform to functions that are not necessarily integrable over the whole real line.
More generally, the Fourier transform also applies to [generalized functions][56] like the [Dirac
delta][57] (and all other [tempered distributions][58]), in which case it is defined by duality
rather than an integral.^{[[3]][59]}

First introduced in [Fourier's][60] Analytical Theory of
Heat.,^{[[4]][61]}^{[[5]][62]}^{[[6]][63]}^{[[7]][64]} the corresponding inversion formula for
functions satisfying [sufficient regularity and decay properties][65] is given by the [Fourier
inversion theorem][66], i.e.,

Inverse transform

───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬───
f ( x ) = ∫ − ∞ ∞ f ^ ( ξ )   e i 2 π ξ x d ξ , ∀ x ∈ R . {\displaystyle f(x)=\int _{-\infty   │Eq.
}^{\infty }{\widehat {f}}(\xi )\ e^{i2\pi \xi x}\,d\xi ,\quad \forall x\in \mathbb {R} .}      │2  
[{\displaystyle f(x)=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }{\widehat {f}}(\xi )\ e^{i2\pi \xi x}\,d\xi     │   
,\quad \forall x\in \mathbb {R} .}]                                                            │   
───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴───

The functions f {\displaystyle f} [{\displaystyle f}] and f ^ {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}}
[{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}}] are referred to as a **Fourier transform pair**.^{[[8]][67]}  A
common notation for designating transform pairs is:^{[[9]][68]} f ( x )   ⟷ F   f ^ ( ξ ) .
{\displaystyle f(x)\ {\stackrel {\mathcal {F}}{\longleftrightarrow }}\ {\widehat {f}}(\xi ).} For
example, the Fourier transform of the delta function is the [constant function][69] ⁠1 {\displaystyle
1} [{\displaystyle 1}]⁠: δ ( x )   ⟷ F   1. {\displaystyle \delta (x)\ {\stackrel {\mathcal
{F}}{\longleftrightarrow }}\ 1.}

### Angular frequency (ω)

When the independent variable (⁠ x {\displaystyle x} [{\displaystyle x}]⁠) represents time (often
denoted by ⁠t {\displaystyle t} [{\displaystyle t}]⁠), the transform variable (⁠ ξ {\displaystyle \xi }
[{\displaystyle \xi }]⁠) represents [frequency][70] (often denoted by ⁠f {\displaystyle f}
[{\displaystyle f}]⁠). For example, if time has the unit [second][71], then frequency has the unit
[hertz][72]. The transform variable can also be written in terms of [angular frequency][73], ⁠ω = 2 π
ξ {\displaystyle \omega =2\pi \xi } [{\displaystyle \omega =2\pi \xi }]⁠, with the unit [radian][74]
per second.

The substitution ξ = ω 2 π {\displaystyle \xi ={\tfrac {\omega }{2\pi }}} [{\displaystyle \xi
={\tfrac {\omega }{2\pi }}}] into **[Eq.1][75]** produces this convention, where function f ^
{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}}] is relabeled ⁠f ^ 1 {\displaystyle
{\widehat {f}}_{1}} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}_{1}}]⁠: f ^ 3 ( ω ) ≜ ∫ − ∞ ∞ f ( x ) ⋅ e − i ω x
d x = f ^ 1 ( ω 2 π ) , f ( x ) = 1 2 π ∫ − ∞ ∞ f ^ 3 ( ω ) ⋅ e i ω x d ω . {\displaystyle
{\begin{aligned}{\widehat {f}}_{3}(\omega )&\triangleq \int _{-\infty }^{\infty }f(x)\cdot
e^{-i\omega x}\,dx={\widehat {f}}_{1}\left({\tfrac {\omega }{2\pi }}\right),\\f(x)&={\frac {1}{2\pi
}}\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }{\widehat {f}}_{3}(\omega )\cdot e^{i\omega x}\,d\omega .\end{aligned}}}
Unlike the **[Eq.1][76]** definition, the Fourier transform is no longer a [unitary
transformation][77], and there is less symmetry between the formulas for the transform and its
inverse. Those properties are restored by splitting the 2 π {\displaystyle 2\pi } [{\displaystyle
2\pi }] factor evenly between the transform and its inverse, which leads to another convention: f ^
2 ( ω ) ≜ 1 2 π ∫ − ∞ ∞ f ( x ) ⋅ e − i ω x d x = 1 2 π     f ^ 1 ( ω 2 π ) , f ( x ) = 1 2 π ∫ − ∞
∞ f ^ 2 ( ω ) ⋅ e i ω x d ω . {\displaystyle {\begin{aligned}{\widehat {f}}_{2}(\omega )&\triangleq
{\frac {1}{\sqrt {2\pi }}}\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }f(x)\cdot e^{-i\omega x}\,dx={\frac {1}{\sqrt
{2\pi }}}\ \ {\widehat {f}}_{1}\left({\tfrac {\omega }{2\pi }}\right),\\f(x)&={\frac {1}{\sqrt {2\pi
}}}\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }{\widehat {f}}_{2}(\omega )\cdot e^{i\omega x}\,d\omega
.\end{aligned}}} Variations of all three conventions can be created by conjugating the
complex-exponential [kernel][78] of both the forward and the reverse transform. The signs must be
opposites.

### Lebesgue integrable functions

See also: [L^{p} space § L^{p} spaces and Lebesgue integrals][79]

A [measurable function][80] f : R → C {\displaystyle f:\mathbb {R} \to \mathbb {C} } [{\displaystyle
f:\mathbb {R} \to \mathbb {C} }] is called (Lebesgue) integrable if the [Lebesgue integral][81] of
its [absolute value][82] is finite: ‖ f ‖ 1 = ∫ R | f ( x ) | d x < ∞ . {\displaystyle
\|f\|_{1}=\int _{\mathbb {R} }|f(x)|\,dx<\infty .} If f {\displaystyle f} [{\displaystyle f}] is
Lebesgue integrable then the Fourier transform, given by **[Eq.1][83]**, is well-defined for all ⁠ξ ∈
R {\displaystyle \xi \in \mathbb {R} } [{\displaystyle \xi \in \mathbb {R} }]⁠.^{[[10]][84]}
Furthermore, f ^ ∈ L ∞ ∩ C 0 ( R ) {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}\in L^{\infty }\cap C_{0}(\mathbb
{R} )} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}\in L^{\infty }\cap C_{0}(\mathbb {R} )}] is bounded,
[uniformly continuous][85] and (by the [Riemann–Lebesgue lemma][86]) [vanishing at infinity][87].
Here C 0 ( R ) {\displaystyle C_{0}(\mathbb {R} )} [{\displaystyle C_{0}(\mathbb {R} )}] denotes the
space of continuous functions on R {\displaystyle \mathbb {R} } [{\displaystyle \mathbb {R} }] that
approach 0 as x approaches positive or negative infinity.

The space L 1 ( R ) {\displaystyle L^{1}(\mathbb {R} )} [{\displaystyle L^{1}(\mathbb {R} )}] is the
space of measurable functions for which the norm ‖ f ‖ 1 {\displaystyle \|f\|_{1}} [{\displaystyle
\|f\|_{1}}] is finite, modulo the [equivalence relation][88] of equality [almost everywhere][89].
The Fourier transform on L 1 ( R ) {\displaystyle L^{1}(\mathbb {R} )} [{\displaystyle L^{1}(\mathbb
{R} )}] is [one-to-one][90]. However, there is no easy characterization of the image, and thus no
easy characterization of the inverse transform. In particular, **[Eq.2][91]** is no longer valid, as
it was stated only under the hypothesis that f ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}] was
"sufficiently nice" (e.g., f ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}] [decays with all
derivatives][92]).

While **[Eq.1][93]** defines the Fourier transform for (complex-valued) functions in ⁠L 1 ( R )
{\displaystyle L^{1}(\mathbb {R} )} [{\displaystyle L^{1}(\mathbb {R} )}]⁠, it is not well-defined
for other integrability classes, most importantly the space of [square-integrable functions][94] ⁠L 2
( R ) {\displaystyle L^{2}(\mathbb {R} )} [{\displaystyle L^{2}(\mathbb {R} )}]⁠. For example, the
function f ( x ) = ( 1 + x 2 ) − 1 / 2 {\displaystyle f(x)=(1+x^{2})^{-1/2}} [{\displaystyle
f(x)=(1+x^{2})^{-1/2}}] is in L 2 {\displaystyle L^{2}} [{\displaystyle L^{2}}] but not L 1
{\displaystyle L^{1}} [{\displaystyle L^{1}}] and therefore the Lebesgue integral **[Eq.1][95]**
does not exist. However, the Fourier transform on the dense subspace L 1 ∩ L 2 ( R ) ⊂ L 2 ( R )
{\displaystyle L^{1}\cap L^{2}(\mathbb {R} )\subset L^{2}(\mathbb {R} )} [{\displaystyle L^{1}\cap
L^{2}(\mathbb {R} )\subset L^{2}(\mathbb {R} )}] admits a unique continuous extension to a [unitary
operator][96] on ⁠L 2 ( R ) {\displaystyle L^{2}(\mathbb {R} )} [{\displaystyle L^{2}(\mathbb {R}
)}]⁠. This extension is important in part because, unlike the case of ⁠L 1 {\displaystyle L^{1}}
[{\displaystyle L^{1}}]⁠, the Fourier transform is an [automorphism][97] of the space ⁠L 2 ( R )
{\displaystyle L^{2}(\mathbb {R} )} [{\displaystyle L^{2}(\mathbb {R} )}]⁠.

In such cases, the Fourier transform can be obtained explicitly by [regularizing][98] the integral,
and then passing to a limit. In practice, the integral is often regarded as an [improper
integral][99] instead of a proper Lebesgue integral, but sometimes for convergence one needs to use
[weak limit][100] or [principal value][101] instead of the (pointwise) limits implicit in an
improper integral. [Titchmarsh (1986)][102] and [Dym & McKean (1985)][103] each gives three rigorous
ways of extending the Fourier transform to square integrable functions using this procedure. A
general principle in working with the L 2 {\displaystyle L^{2}} [{\displaystyle L^{2}}] Fourier
transform is that finite linear combinations of Gaussians are dense in ⁠L 1 ∩ L 2 {\displaystyle
L^{1}\cap L^{2}} [{\displaystyle L^{1}\cap L^{2}}]⁠, and the various features of the Fourier
transform, such as its unitarity, are easily inferred for Gaussians. Many of the properties of the
Fourier transform can then be proven from two facts about Gaussians:^{[[11]][104]}
* that e − π x 2 {\displaystyle e^{-\pi x^{2}}} [{\displaystyle e^{-\pi x^{2}}}] is its own Fourier
  transform; and
* that the [Gaussian integral][105] ⁠∫ − ∞ ∞ e − π x 2 d x = 1 {\displaystyle \textstyle \int
  _{-\infty }^{\infty }e^{-\pi x^{2}}\,dx=1} [{\displaystyle \textstyle \int _{-\infty }^{\infty
  }e^{-\pi x^{2}}\,dx=1}]⁠.

A feature of the L 1 {\displaystyle L^{1}} [{\displaystyle L^{1}}] Fourier transform is that it is a
homomorphism of Banach algebras from L 1 {\displaystyle L^{1}} [{\displaystyle L^{1}}] equipped with
the convolution operation to the Banach algebra of continuous functions under the L ∞ {\displaystyle
L^{\infty }} [{\displaystyle L^{\infty }}] (supremum) norm. The conventions chosen in this article
are those of [harmonic analysis][106], such that the Fourier transform is both [unitary][107] on ⁠L 2
{\displaystyle L^{2}} [{\displaystyle L^{2}}]⁠ and an algebra homomorphism from ⁠1 {\displaystyle {1}}
[{\displaystyle {1}}]⁠ to ⁠L ∞ {\displaystyle L^{\infty }} [{\displaystyle L^{\infty }}]⁠, without
renormalizing the Lebesgue measure.^{[[12]][108]}

## Background

### History

Main articles: [Fourier analysis § History][109], and [Fourier series § History][110]

In 1822, Fourier claimed (see [Joseph Fourier § The Analytic Theory of Heat][111]) that any
function, whether continuous or discontinuous, can be expanded into a series of sines.^{[[13]][112]}
That important work was corrected and expanded upon by others to provide the foundation for the
various forms of the Fourier transform used since.

### Complex sinusoids

The red [sinusoid][113] can be described by peak amplitude (1), peak-to-peak (2), [RMS][114] (3),
and [wavelength][115] (4). The red and blue sinusoids have a phase difference of θ.

In general, the coefficients f ^ ( ξ ) {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi )} [{\displaystyle
{\widehat {f}}(\xi )}] are complex numbers, which have two equivalent forms (see [Euler's
formula][116]): f ^ ( ξ ) = A e i θ ⏟ polar coordinate form = A cos ⁡( θ ) + i A sin ⁡( θ ) ⏟
rectangular coordinate form . {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi )=\underbrace {Ae^{i\theta }}
_{\text{polar coordinate form}}=\underbrace {A\cos(\theta )+iA\sin(\theta )} _{\text{rectangular
coordinate form}}.}

The product with e i 2 π ξ x {\displaystyle e^{i2\pi \xi x}} [{\displaystyle e^{i2\pi \xi x}}]
(**[Eq.2][117]**) has these forms: f ^ ( ξ ) ⋅ e i 2 π ξ x = A e i θ ⋅ e i 2 π ξ x = A e i ( 2 π ξ x
+ θ ) ⏟ polar coordinate form = A cos ⁡( 2 π ξ x + θ ) + i A sin ⁡( 2 π ξ x + θ ) ⏟ rectangular
coordinate form , {\displaystyle {\begin{aligned}{\widehat {f}}(\xi )\cdot e^{i2\pi \xi
x}&=Ae^{i\theta }\cdot e^{i2\pi \xi x}\\[6pt]&=\underbrace {Ae^{i(2\pi \xi x+\theta )}}
_{\text{polar coordinate form}}\\[6pt]&=\underbrace {A\cos(2\pi \xi x+\theta )+iA\sin(2\pi \xi
x+\theta )} _{\text{rectangular coordinate form}},\end{aligned}}} which conveys both
[amplitude][118] and [phase][119] of frequency ⁠ξ {\displaystyle \xi } [{\displaystyle \xi }]⁠.
Likewise, the intuitive interpretation of **[Eq.1][120]** is that multiplying f ( x ) {\displaystyle
f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}] by e − i 2 π ξ x {\displaystyle e^{-i2\pi \xi x}} [{\displaystyle
e^{-i2\pi \xi x}}] has the effect of subtracting ξ {\displaystyle \xi } [{\displaystyle \xi }] from
every frequency component of function ⁠f ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}]⁠.^{[[note
5]][121]} Only the component that was at frequency ξ {\displaystyle \xi } [{\displaystyle \xi }] can
produce a non-zero value of the infinite integral, because (at least formally) all the other shifted
components are oscillatory and integrate to zero (see [§ Example][122]).

It is noteworthy how easily the product was simplified using the polar form, and how easily the
rectangular form was deduced by an application of Euler's formula.

### Negative frequency

See also: [Negative frequency § Simplifying the Fourier transform][123]

Euler's formula introduces the possibility of negative ⁠ξ {\displaystyle \xi } [{\displaystyle \xi
}]⁠. **[Eq.1][124]** is defined ⁠∀ ξ ∈ R {\displaystyle \forall \xi \in \mathbb {R} } [{\displaystyle
\forall \xi \in \mathbb {R} }]⁠. Only certain complex-valued f ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)}
[{\displaystyle f(x)}] have transforms ⁠f ^ = 0 ,   ∀   ξ < 0 {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}=0,\
\forall \ \xi <0} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}=0,\ \forall \ \xi <0}]⁠. (See [Analytic
signal][125]; a simple example is ⁠e i 2 π ξ 0 x   ( ξ 0 > 0 ) {\displaystyle e^{i2\pi \xi _{0}x}\
(\xi _{0}>0)} [{\displaystyle e^{i2\pi \xi _{0}x}\ (\xi _{0}>0)}]⁠.)  But negative frequency is
necessary to characterize all other complex-valued ⁠f ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)} [{\displaystyle
f(x)}]⁠, found in [signal processing][126], [partial differential equations][127], [radar][128],
[nonlinear optics][129], [quantum mechanics][130], and others.

For a real-valued ⁠f ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}]⁠, **[Eq.1][131]** has the
symmetry property f ^ ( − ξ ) = f ^ ∗ ( ξ ) {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(-\xi )={\widehat
{f}}^{*}(\xi )} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(-\xi )={\widehat {f}}^{*}(\xi )}] (see
[§ Conjugation][132] below). This redundancy enables **[Eq.2][133]** to distinguish f ( x ) = cos ⁡(
2 π ξ 0 x ) {\displaystyle f(x)=\cos(2\pi \xi _{0}x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)=\cos(2\pi \xi _{0}x)}]
from ⁠e i 2 π ξ 0 x {\displaystyle e^{i2\pi \xi _{0}x}} [{\displaystyle e^{i2\pi \xi _{0}x}}]⁠. But it
cannot determine the actual sign of ⁠ξ 0 {\displaystyle \xi _{0}} [{\displaystyle \xi _{0}}]⁠, because
cos ⁡( 2 π ξ 0 x ) {\displaystyle \cos(2\pi \xi _{0}x)} [{\displaystyle \cos(2\pi \xi _{0}x)}] and
cos ⁡( 2 π ( − ξ 0 ) x ) {\displaystyle \cos(2\pi (-\xi _{0})x)} [{\displaystyle \cos(2\pi (-\xi
_{0})x)}] are indistinguishable on just the real numbers line.

### Fourier transform for periodic functions

The Fourier transform of a periodic function cannot be defined using the integral formula directly.
In order for integral in **[Eq.1][134]** to be defined the function must be [absolutely
integrable][135]. Instead it is common to use [Fourier series][136]. It is possible to extend the
definition to include periodic functions by viewing them as [tempered distributions][137].

This makes it possible to see a connection between the [Fourier series][138] and the Fourier
transform for periodic functions that have a [convergent Fourier series][139]. If f ( x )
{\displaystyle f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}] is a [periodic function][140], with period ⁠P
{\displaystyle P} [{\displaystyle P}]⁠, that has a convergent Fourier series, then: f ^ ( ξ ) = ∑ n =
− ∞ ∞ c n ⋅ δ ( ξ − n P ) , {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi )=\sum _{n=-\infty }^{\infty
}c_{n}\cdot \delta \left(\xi -{\tfrac {n}{P}}\right),} where c n {\displaystyle c_{n}}
[{\displaystyle c_{n}}] are the Fourier series coefficients of ⁠f {\displaystyle f} [{\displaystyle
f}]⁠, and δ {\displaystyle \delta } [{\displaystyle \delta }] is the [Dirac delta function][141]. In
other words, the Fourier transform is a [Dirac comb][142] function whose teeth are multiplied by the
Fourier series coefficients.

### Sampling the Fourier transform

For broader coverage of this topic, see [Poisson summation formula][143].

The Fourier transform of an [integrable][144] function f {\displaystyle f} [{\displaystyle f}] can
be sampled at regular intervals of arbitrary length ⁠1 / P {\displaystyle 1/P} [{\displaystyle 1/P}]⁠.
These samples can be deduced from one cycle of a periodic function ⁠f P {\displaystyle f_{P}}
[{\displaystyle f_{P}}]⁠, which has [Fourier series][145] coefficients proportional to those samples
by the [Poisson summation formula][146]: f P ( x ) ≜ ∑ n = − ∞ ∞ f ( x + n P ) = 1 P ∑ k = − ∞ ∞ f ^
( k P ) e i 2 π k P x , ∀ k ∈ Z . {\displaystyle f_{P}(x)\triangleq \sum _{n=-\infty }^{\infty
}f(x+nP)={\frac {1}{P}}\sum _{k=-\infty }^{\infty }{\widehat {f}}\left({\tfrac
{k}{P}}\right)e^{i2\pi {\frac {k}{P}}x},\quad \forall k\in \mathbb {Z} .}

The integrability of f {\displaystyle f} [{\displaystyle f}] ensures the [periodic summation][147]
converges. Therefore, the samples f ^ ( k P ) {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}({\tfrac {k}{P}})}
[{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}({\tfrac {k}{P}})}] can be determined by Fourier series analysis: f ^
( k P ) = ∫ P f P ( x ) ⋅ e − i 2 π k P x d x . {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}\left({\tfrac
{k}{P}}\right)=\int _{P}f_{P}(x)\cdot e^{-i2\pi {\frac {k}{P}}x}\,dx.}

When f ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}] has [compact support][148], f P ( x )
{\displaystyle f_{P}(x)} [{\displaystyle f_{P}(x)}] has a finite number of terms within the interval
of integration. When f ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}] does not have compact
support, numerical evaluation of f P ( x ) {\displaystyle f_{P}(x)} [{\displaystyle f_{P}(x)}]
requires an approximation, such as tapering f ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}] or
truncating the number of terms.

## Units

See also: [Spectral density § Units][149]

The frequency variable must have inverse units to the units of the original function's domain
(typically named t {\displaystyle t} [{\displaystyle t}] or ⁠x {\displaystyle x} [{\displaystyle
x}]⁠). For example, if t {\displaystyle t} [{\displaystyle t}] is measured in seconds, ξ
{\displaystyle \xi } [{\displaystyle \xi }] should be in cycles per second or [hertz][150]. If the
scale of time is in units of 2 π {\displaystyle 2\pi } [{\displaystyle 2\pi }] seconds, then another
Greek letter ω {\displaystyle \omega } [{\displaystyle \omega }] is typically used instead to
represent [angular frequency][151] (where ⁠ω = 2 π ξ {\displaystyle \omega =2\pi \xi }
[{\displaystyle \omega =2\pi \xi }]⁠) in units of [radians][152] per second. If using x
{\displaystyle x} [{\displaystyle x}] for units of length, then ξ {\displaystyle \xi }
[{\displaystyle \xi }] must be in inverse length, e.g., [wavenumbers][153]. That is to say, there
are two versions of the real line: one that is the [range][154] of t {\displaystyle t}
[{\displaystyle t}] and measured in units of ⁠t {\displaystyle t} [{\displaystyle t}]⁠, and the other
that is the range of ξ {\displaystyle \xi } [{\displaystyle \xi }] and measured in inverse units to
the units of ⁠t {\displaystyle t} [{\displaystyle t}]⁠. These two distinct versions of the real line
cannot be equated with each other. Therefore, the Fourier transform goes from one space of functions
to a different space of functions: functions that have a different domain of definition.

In general, ξ {\displaystyle \xi } [{\displaystyle \xi }] must always be taken to be a [linear
form][155] on the space of its domain, which is to say that the second real line is the [dual
space][156] of the first real line. (See the article [Linear algebra][157] for a more formal
explanation and for more details.) This point of view becomes essential in generalizations of the
Fourier transform to general [symmetry groups][158], including the case of Fourier series.

That there is no one preferred way (often, one says "no canonical way") to compare the two versions
of the real line that are involved in the Fourier transform—fixing the units on one line does not
force the scale of the units on the other line—is the reason for the plethora of rival conventions
on the definition of the Fourier transform. The various definitions resulting from different choices
of units differ by various constants.

In other conventions, the Fourier transform has i in the exponent instead of −i, and vice versa for
the inversion formula. This convention is common in modern physics^{[[14]][159]} and is the default
for [Wolfram Alpha][160], and does not mean that the frequency has become negative, since there is
no canonical definition of positivity for frequency of a complex wave. It simply means that f ^ ( ξ
) {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi )} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi )}] is the amplitude of
the wave ⁠ e − i 2 π ξ x {\displaystyle e^{-i2\pi \xi x}} [{\displaystyle e^{-i2\pi \xi x}}]⁠ instead
of the wave  e i 2 π ξ x {\displaystyle e^{i2\pi \xi x}} [{\displaystyle e^{i2\pi \xi x}}] (the
former, with its minus sign, is often seen in the time dependence for [sinusoidal plane-wave
solutions of the electromagnetic wave equation][161], or in the [time dependence for quantum wave
functions][162]). Many of the identities involving the Fourier transform remain valid in those
conventions, provided all terms that explicitly involve i have it replaced by −i. In [electrical
engineering][163] the letter j is typically used for the [imaginary unit][164] instead of i because
i is used for current.

When using [dimensionless units][165], the constant factors might not be written in the transform
definition. For instance, in [probability theory][166], the characteristic function Φ of the
probability density function ⁠f {\displaystyle f} [{\displaystyle f}]⁠ of a random variable ⁠X
{\displaystyle X} [{\displaystyle X}]⁠ of continuous type is defined without a negative sign in the
exponential, and since the units of ⁠x {\displaystyle x} [{\displaystyle x}]⁠ are ignored, there is no
⁠2 π {\displaystyle 2\pi } [{\displaystyle 2\pi }]⁠ either: φ ( λ ) = ∫ − ∞ ∞ f ( x ) e i λ x d x .
{\displaystyle \varphi (\lambda )=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }f(x)e^{i\lambda x}\,dx.}

In probability theory and mathematical statistics, the use of the Fourier—Stieltjes transform is
preferred, because many random variables are not of continuous type, and do not possess a density
function, and one must treat not functions but [distributions][167], i.e., measures that possess
"atoms".

From the higher point of view of [group characters][168], which is much more abstract, all these
arbitrary choices disappear, as will be explained in the later section of this article, which treats
the notion of the Fourier transform of a function on a [locally compact abelian group][169].

## Properties

Let f ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}] and h ( x ) {\displaystyle h(x)}
[{\displaystyle h(x)}] represent integrable functions [Lebesgue-measurable][170] on the real line
satisfying: ∫ − ∞ ∞ | f ( x ) | d x < ∞ . {\displaystyle \int _{-\infty }^{\infty }|f(x)|\,dx<\infty
.} We denote the Fourier transforms of these functions as f ^ ( ξ ) {\displaystyle {\widehat
{f}}(\xi )} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi )}] and h ^ ( ξ ) {\displaystyle {\widehat {h}}(\xi
)} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {h}}(\xi )}] respectively.

### Basic properties

The Fourier transform has the following basic properties:^{[[15]][171]}

#### Linearity

a   f ( x ) + b   h ( x )     ⟺ F     a   f ^ ( ξ ) + b   h ^ ( ξ ) ;   a , b ∈ C {\displaystyle a\
f(x)+b\ h(x)\ \ {\stackrel {\mathcal {F}}{\Longleftrightarrow }}\ \ a\ {\widehat {f}}(\xi )+b\
{\widehat {h}}(\xi );\quad \ a,b\in \mathbb {C} }

#### Time shifting

f ( x − x 0 )     ⟺ F     e − i 2 π x 0 ξ   f ^ ( ξ ) ;   x 0 ∈ R {\displaystyle f(x-x_{0})\ \
{\stackrel {\mathcal {F}}{\Longleftrightarrow }}\ \ e^{-i2\pi x_{0}\xi }\ {\widehat {f}}(\xi );\quad
\ x_{0}\in \mathbb {R} }

#### Frequency shifting

e i 2 π ξ 0 x f ( x )     ⟺ F     f ^ ( ξ − ξ 0 ) ;   ξ 0 ∈ R {\displaystyle e^{i2\pi \xi
_{0}x}f(x)\ \ {\stackrel {\mathcal {F}}{\Longleftrightarrow }}\ \ {\widehat {f}}(\xi -\xi
_{0});\quad \ \xi _{0}\in \mathbb {R} }

#### Time scaling

f ( a x )     ⟺ F     1 | a | f ^ ( ξ a ) ;   a ≠ 0 {\displaystyle f(ax)\ \ {\stackrel {\mathcal
{F}}{\Longleftrightarrow }}\ \ {\frac {1}{|a|}}{\widehat {f}}\left({\frac {\xi }{a}}\right);\quad \
a\neq 0} The case a = − 1 {\displaystyle a=-1} [{\displaystyle a=-1}] leads to the time-reversal
property: f ( − x )     ⟺ F     f ^ ( − ξ ) {\displaystyle f(-x)\ \ {\stackrel {\mathcal
{F}}{\Longleftrightarrow }}\ \ {\widehat {f}}(-\xi )}

f ( t ) {\displaystyle \scriptstyle f(t)} [{\displaystyle \scriptstyle f(t)}]

g ( t ) {\displaystyle \scriptstyle g(t)} [{\displaystyle \scriptstyle g(t)}]

ω {\displaystyle \scriptstyle \omega } [{\displaystyle \scriptstyle \omega }]

The transform of an even-symmetric real-valued function ⁠f ( t ) = f RE {\displaystyle

function (⁠ f ^ RE {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}\!_{_{\text{RE}}}} [{\displaystyle {\widehat
{f}}\!_{_{\text{RE}}}}]⁠). The time-shift, ⁠g ( t ) = g RE + g RO {\displaystyle

creates an imaginary component, ⁠i   g   ^ IO {\displaystyle i\ {\widehat {g\ \!}}_{_{\text{IO}}}}
[{\displaystyle i\ {\widehat {g\ \!}}_{_{\text{IO}}}}]⁠. (See [§ Symmetry][172].)

#### Symmetry

When the real and imaginary parts of a complex function are decomposed into their [even and odd
parts][173], there are four components, denoted below by the subscripts RE, RO, IE, and IO. And
there is a one-to-one mapping between the four components of a complex time function and the four
components of its complex frequency transform:^{[[16]][174]}

  T i m e   d o m a i n f = f RE + f RO + i   f IE + i   f IO ⏟ ⇕ F ⇕ F     ⇕ F     ⇕ F     ⇕ F F r
  e q u e n c y   d o m a i n f ^ = f ^ RE + i   f ^ IO ⏞ + i   f ^ IE + f ^ RO {\displaystyle
  {\begin{array}{rlcccccccc}{\mathsf {Time\ domain}}&f&=&f_{_{\text{RE}}}&+&f_{_{\text{RO}}}&+&i\

  {F}}&&{\Bigg \Updownarrow }{\mathcal {F}}&&\ \ {\Bigg \Updownarrow }{\mathcal {F}}&&\ \ {\Bigg

  {f}}\!_{_{\text{IO}}}} &+&i\ {\widehat {f}}\!_{_{\text{IE}}}&+&{\widehat
  {f}}\!_{_{\text{RO}}}\end{array}}} [{\displaystyle {\begin{array}{rlcccccccc}{\mathsf {Time\
  domain}}&f&=&f_{_{\text{RE}}}&+&f_{_{\text{RO}}}&+&i\ f_{_{\text{IE}}}&+&\underbrace {i\
  f_{_{\text{IO}}}} \\&{\Bigg \Updownarrow }{\mathcal {F}}&&{\Bigg \Updownarrow }{\mathcal {F}}&&\ \
  {\Bigg \Updownarrow }{\mathcal {F}}&&\ \ {\Bigg \Updownarrow }{\mathcal {F}}&&\ \ {\Bigg
  \Updownarrow }{\mathcal {F}}\\{\mathsf {Frequency\ domain}}&{\widehat {f}}&=&{\widehat
  {f}}\!_{_{\text{RE}}}&+&\overbrace {i\ {\widehat {f}}\!_{_{\text{IO}}}} &+&i\ {\widehat
  {f}}\!_{_{\text{IE}}}&+&{\widehat {f}}\!_{_{\text{RO}}}\end{array}}}]

From this, various relationships are apparent, for example:
* The transform of a real-valued function (⁠ f RE + f RO {\displaystyle
  f_{_{\text{RE}}}+f_{_{\text{RO}}}} [{\displaystyle f_{_{\text{RE}}}+f_{_{\text{RO}}}}]⁠) is the
  [conjugate symmetric][175] function ⁠f ^ RE + i   f ^ IO {\displaystyle {\widehat
  {f}}\!_{_{\text{RE}}}+i\ {\widehat {f}}\!_{_{\text{IO}}}} [{\displaystyle {\widehat
  {f}}\!_{_{\text{RE}}}+i\ {\widehat {f}}\!_{_{\text{IO}}}}]⁠. Conversely, a conjugate symmetric
  transform implies a real-valued time-domain.
* The transform of an imaginary-valued function (⁠ i   f IE + i   f IO {\displaystyle i\
  f_{_{\text{IE}}}+i\ f_{_{\text{IO}}}} [{\displaystyle i\ f_{_{\text{IE}}}+i\ f_{_{\text{IO}}}}]⁠)
  is the [conjugate antisymmetric][176] function ⁠f ^ RO + i   f ^ IE {\displaystyle {\widehat
  {f}}\!_{_{\text{RO}}}+i\ {\widehat {f}}\!_{_{\text{IE}}}} [{\displaystyle {\widehat
  {f}}\!_{_{\text{RO}}}+i\ {\widehat {f}}\!_{_{\text{IE}}}}]⁠, and the converse is true.
* The transform of a [conjugate symmetric][177] function ( f RE + i   f IO ) {\displaystyle
  (f_{_{\text{RE}}}+i\ f_{_{\text{IO}}})} [{\displaystyle (f_{_{\text{RE}}}+i\ f_{_{\text{IO}}})}]
  is the real-valued function ⁠f ^ RE + f ^ RO {\displaystyle {\widehat
  {f}}\!_{_{\text{RE}}}+{\widehat {f}}\!_{_{\text{RO}}}} [{\displaystyle {\widehat
  {f}}\!_{_{\text{RE}}}+{\widehat {f}}\!_{_{\text{RO}}}}]⁠, and the converse is true.
* The transform of a [conjugate antisymmetric][178] function ( f RO + i   f IE ) {\displaystyle
  (f_{_{\text{RO}}}+i\ f_{_{\text{IE}}})} [{\displaystyle (f_{_{\text{RO}}}+i\ f_{_{\text{IE}}})}]
  is the imaginary-valued function ⁠i   f ^ IE + i   f ^ IO {\displaystyle i\ {\widehat
  {f}}\!_{_{\text{IE}}}+i\ {\widehat {f}}\!_{_{\text{IO}}}} [{\displaystyle i\ {\widehat
  {f}}\!_{_{\text{IE}}}+i\ {\widehat {f}}\!_{_{\text{IO}}}}]⁠, and the converse is true.

#### Conjugation

( f ( x ) ) ∗     ⟺ F     ( f ^ ( − ξ ) ) ∗ {\displaystyle {\bigl (}f(x){\bigr )}^{*}\ \ {\stackrel
{\mathcal {F}}{\Longleftrightarrow }}\ \ \left({\widehat {f}}(-\xi )\right)^{*}} (Note: the ⁠∗
{\displaystyle *} [{\displaystyle *}]⁠ denotes [complex conjugation][179].)

In particular, if f {\displaystyle f} [{\displaystyle f}] is real, then f ^ {\displaystyle {\widehat
{f}}} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}}] is [conjugate symmetric][180] (a.k.a. [Hermitian
function][181]): f ^ ( − ξ ) = ( f ^ ( ξ ) ) ∗ . {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(-\xi )={\bigl
(}{\widehat {f}}(\xi ){\bigr )}^{*}.}

If f {\displaystyle f} [{\displaystyle f}] is purely imaginary, then f ^ {\displaystyle {\widehat
{f}}} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}}] is [odd symmetric][182]: f ^ ( − ξ ) = − ( f ^ ( ξ ) ) ∗ .
{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(-\xi )=-({\widehat {f}}(\xi ))^{*}.}

#### Real and imaginary parts

Re ⁡{ f ( x ) }     ⟺ F     1 2 ( f ^ ( ξ ) + ( f ^ ( − ξ ) ) ∗ ) {\displaystyle \operatorname {Re}
\{f(x)\}\ \ {\stackrel {\mathcal {F}}{\Longleftrightarrow }}\ \ {\tfrac {1}{2}}\left({\widehat
{f}}(\xi )+{\bigl (}{\widehat {f}}(-\xi ){\bigr )}^{*}\right)} Im ⁡{ f ( x ) }     ⟺ F     1 2 i ( f
^ ( ξ ) − ( f ^ ( − ξ ) ) ∗ ) {\displaystyle \operatorname {Im} \{f(x)\}\ \ {\stackrel {\mathcal
{F}}{\Longleftrightarrow }}\ \ {\tfrac {1}{2i}}\left({\widehat {f}}(\xi )-{\bigl (}{\widehat
{f}}(-\xi ){\bigr )}^{*}\right)}

#### Zero frequency component

Substituting ξ = 0 {\displaystyle \xi =0} [{\displaystyle \xi =0}] in the definition, we obtain: f ^
( 0 ) = ∫ − ∞ ∞ f ( x ) d x . {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(0)=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }f(x)\,dx.}

The integral of f {\displaystyle f} [{\displaystyle f}] over its domain is the total mass or [DC
bias][183] of the function.

### Uniform continuity and the Riemann–Lebesgue lemma

The Fourier transform may be defined in some cases for non-integrable functions, but the Fourier
transforms of integrable functions have several strong properties.

The Fourier transform f ^ {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}}] of any
integrable function f {\displaystyle f} [{\displaystyle f}] is [uniformly continuous][184]
and^{[[17]][185]}^{[[18]][186]} ‖ f ^ ‖ ∞ ≤ ‖ f ‖ 1 {\displaystyle \left\|{\widehat
{f}}\right\|_{\infty }\leq \left\|f\right\|_{1}}

By the [Riemann–Lebesgue lemma][187],^{[[19]][188]} f ^ ( ξ ) → 0  as  | ξ | → ∞ . {\displaystyle
{\widehat {f}}(\xi )\to 0{\text{ as }}|\xi |\to \infty .}

However, f ^ {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}}] need not be integrable.
For example, the Fourier transform of the [rectangular function][189], which is integrable, is the
[sinc function][190], which is not [Lebesgue integrable][191], because its [improper integrals][192]
behave analogously to the [alternating harmonic series][193], in converging to a sum without being
[absolutely convergent][194].

It is not generally possible to write the inverse transform as a [Lebesgue integral][195]. However,
when both f {\displaystyle f} [{\displaystyle f}] and f ^ {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}}
[{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}}] are integrable, the inverse equality f ( x ) = ∫ − ∞ ∞ f ^ ( ξ ) e
i 2 π x ξ d ξ {\displaystyle f(x)=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }{\widehat {f}}(\xi )e^{i2\pi x\xi
}\,d\xi } holds for almost every x. As a result, the Fourier transform is [injective][196] on
[L¹(**R**)][197].

### Plancherel theorem and Parseval's theorem

Main articles: [Plancherel theorem][198] and [Parseval's theorem][199]

Let ⁠f ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}]⁠ and ⁠g ( x ) {\displaystyle g(x)}
[{\displaystyle g(x)}]⁠ be integrable, and let ⁠f ^ {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}} [{\displaystyle
{\widehat {f}}}]⁠ and ⁠g ^ {\displaystyle {\widehat {g}}} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {g}}}]⁠ be their
Fourier transforms. If ⁠f ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}]⁠ and ⁠g ( x )
{\displaystyle g(x)} [{\displaystyle g(x)}]⁠ are also [square-integrable][200], then the Parseval
formula follows:^{[[20]][201]} ⟨ f , g ⟩ L 2 = ∫ − ∞ ∞ f ( x ) g ( x ) ¯ d x = ∫ − ∞ ∞ f ^ ( ξ ) g ^
( ξ ) ¯ d ξ , {\displaystyle \langle f,g\rangle _{L^{2}}=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }f(x){\overline
{g(x)}}\,dx=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }{\widehat {f}}(\xi ){\overline {{\widehat {g}}(\xi )}}\,d\xi
,} where the bar denotes [complex conjugation][202].

The [Plancherel theorem][203], which follows from the above, states that^{[[21]][204]} ‖ f ‖ L 2 2 =
∫ − ∞ ∞ | f ( x ) | 2 d x = ∫ − ∞ ∞ | f ^ ( ξ ) | 2 d ξ . {\displaystyle \|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\int
_{-\infty }^{\infty }\left|f(x)\right|^{2}\,dx=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }\left|{\widehat {f}}(\xi
)\right|^{2}\,d\xi .}

Plancherel's theorem makes it possible to extend the Fourier transform, by a continuity argument, to
a [unitary operator][205] on ⁠L 2 ( R ) {\displaystyle L^{2}(\mathbb {R} )} [{\displaystyle
L^{2}(\mathbb {R} )}]⁠. On ⁠L 1 ( R ) ∩ L 2 ( R ) {\displaystyle L^{1}(\mathbb {R} )\cap L^{2}(\mathbb
{R} )} [{\displaystyle L^{1}(\mathbb {R} )\cap L^{2}(\mathbb {R} )}]⁠, this extension agrees with
original Fourier transform defined on ⁠L 1 ( R ) {\displaystyle L^{1}(\mathbb {R} )} [{\displaystyle
L^{1}(\mathbb {R} )}]⁠, thus enlarging the domain of the Fourier transform to ⁠L 1 ( R ) + L 2 ( R )
{\displaystyle L^{1}(\mathbb {R} )+L^{2}(\mathbb {R} )} [{\displaystyle L^{1}(\mathbb {R}
)+L^{2}(\mathbb {R} )}]⁠ (and consequently to ⁠L p ( R ) {\displaystyle L^{p}(\mathbb {R} )}
[{\displaystyle L^{p}(\mathbb {R} )}]⁠ for ⁠1 ≤ p ≤ 2 {\displaystyle 1\leq p\leq 2} [{\displaystyle
1\leq p\leq 2}]⁠). Plancherel's theorem has the interpretation in the sciences that the Fourier
transform preserves the [energy][206] of the original quantity. The terminology of these formulas is
not quite standardised. [Parseval's theorem][207] was proved only for Fourier series, and was first
proved by Lyapunov. But Parseval's formula makes sense for the Fourier transform as well, and so
even though in the context of the Fourier transform it was proved by Plancherel, it is still often
referred to as Parseval's formula, or Parseval's relation, or even Parseval's theorem.

See [Pontryagin duality][208] for a general formulation of this concept in the context of locally
compact abelian groups.

### Convolution theorem

Main article: [Convolution theorem][209]

The Fourier transform translates between [convolution][210] and multiplication of functions. If ⁠f (
x ) {\displaystyle f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}]⁠ and ⁠g ( x ) {\displaystyle g(x)} [{\displaystyle
g(x)}]⁠ are integrable functions with Fourier transforms ⁠f ^ {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}}
[{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}}]⁠ and ⁠g ^ ( ξ ) {\displaystyle {\widehat {g}}(\xi )} [{\displaystyle
{\widehat {g}}(\xi )}]⁠ respectively, then the Fourier transform of the convolution is given by the
product of the Fourier transforms ⁠f ^ {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}} [{\displaystyle {\widehat
{f}}}]⁠ and ⁠g ^ {\displaystyle {\widehat {g}}} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {g}}}]⁠ (under other
conventions for the definition of the Fourier transform a constant factor may appear).

This means that if: h ( x ) = ( f ∗ g ) ( x ) = ∫ − ∞ ∞ f ( y ) g ( x − y ) d y , {\displaystyle
h(x)=(f*g)(x)=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }f(y)g(x-y)\,dy,} where ∗ denotes the convolution operation,
then: h ^ ( ξ ) = f ^ ( ξ ) g ^ ( ξ ) . {\displaystyle {\widehat {h}}(\xi )={\widehat {f}}(\xi
)\,{\widehat {g}}(\xi ).}

In [linear time invariant (LTI) system theory][211], it is common to interpret ⁠g ( x )
{\displaystyle g(x)} [{\displaystyle g(x)}]⁠ as the [impulse response][212] of an LTI system with
input ⁠f ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}]⁠ and output ⁠h ( x ) {\displaystyle h(x)}
[{\displaystyle h(x)}]⁠, since substituting the [unit impulse][213] for ⁠f ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)}
[{\displaystyle f(x)}]⁠ yields ⁠h ( x ) = g ( x ) {\displaystyle h(x)=g(x)} [{\displaystyle
h(x)=g(x)}]⁠. In this case, ⁠g ^ ( ξ ) {\displaystyle {\widehat {g}}(\xi )} [{\displaystyle {\widehat
{g}}(\xi )}]⁠ represents the [frequency response][214] of the system.

Conversely, if ⁠f ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}]⁠ can be decomposed as the product
of two square integrable functions ⁠p ( x ) {\displaystyle p(x)} [{\displaystyle p(x)}]⁠ and ⁠q ( x )
{\displaystyle q(x)} [{\displaystyle q(x)}]⁠, then the Fourier transform of ⁠f ( x ) {\displaystyle
f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}]⁠ is given by the convolution of the respective Fourier transforms ⁠p ^ (
ξ ) {\displaystyle {\widehat {p}}(\xi )} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {p}}(\xi )}]⁠ and ⁠q ^ ( ξ )
{\displaystyle {\widehat {q}}(\xi )} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {q}}(\xi )}]⁠.

### Cross-correlation theorem

Main articles: [Cross-correlation][215] and [Wiener–Khinchin theorem][216]

In an analogous manner, it can be shown that if ⁠h ( x ) {\displaystyle h(x)} [{\displaystyle h(x)}]⁠
is the [cross-correlation][217] of ⁠f ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}]⁠ and ⁠g ( x )
{\displaystyle g(x)} [{\displaystyle g(x)}]⁠: h ( x ) = ( f ⋆ g ) ( x ) = ∫ − ∞ ∞ f ( y ) ¯ g ( x + y
) d y {\displaystyle h(x)=(f\star g)(x)=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }{\overline {f(y)}}g(x+y)\,dy} then
the Fourier transform of ⁠h ( x ) {\displaystyle h(x)} [{\displaystyle h(x)}]⁠ is: h ^ ( ξ ) = f ^ ( ξ
) ¯ g ^ ( ξ ) . {\displaystyle {\widehat {h}}(\xi )={\overline {{\widehat {f}}(\xi )}}\,{\widehat
{g}}(\xi ).}

As a special case, the [autocorrelation][218] of function ⁠f ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)}
[{\displaystyle f(x)}]⁠ is: h ( x ) = ( f ⋆ f ) ( x ) = ∫ − ∞ ∞ f ( y ) ¯ f ( x + y ) d y
{\displaystyle h(x)=(f\star f)(x)=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }{\overline {f(y)}}f(x+y)\,dy} for which
h ^ ( ξ ) = f ^ ( ξ ) ¯ f ^ ( ξ ) = | f ^ ( ξ ) | 2 . {\displaystyle {\widehat {h}}(\xi )={\overline
{{\widehat {f}}(\xi )}}{\widehat {f}}(\xi )=\left|{\widehat {f}}(\xi )\right|^{2}.}

### Differentiation

Suppose f(x) is differentiable [almost everywhere][219], and both ⁠f {\displaystyle f}
[{\displaystyle f}]⁠ and its derivative ⁠f ′ {\displaystyle f'} [{\displaystyle f'}]⁠ are integrable
(in ⁠L 1 ( R ) {\displaystyle L^{1}(\mathbb {R} )} [{\displaystyle L^{1}(\mathbb {R} )}]⁠). Then the
Fourier transform of the derivative is given by f ′ ^ ( ξ ) = F { d d x f ( x ) } = i 2 π ξ f ^ ( ξ
) . {\displaystyle {\widehat {f'}}(\xi )={\mathcal {F}}\left\{{\frac {d}{dx}}f(x)\right\}=i2\pi \xi
{\widehat {f}}(\xi ).} More generally, the Fourier transformation of the ⁠n {\displaystyle n}
[{\displaystyle n}]⁠th derivative ⁠f ( n ) {\displaystyle f^{(n)}} [{\displaystyle f^{(n)}}]⁠ is given
by f ( n ) ^ ( ξ ) = F { d n d x n f ( x ) } = ( i 2 π ξ ) n f ^ ( ξ ) . {\displaystyle {\widehat
{f^{(n)}}}(\xi )={\mathcal {F}}\left\{{\frac {d^{n}}{dx^{n}}}f(x)\right\}=(i2\pi \xi )^{n}{\widehat
{f}}(\xi ).}

Analogously, ⁠F − 1 { d n d ξ n f ^ ( ξ ) } = ( − i 2 π x ) n f ( x ) {\displaystyle {\mathcal
{F}}^{-1}\left\{{\frac {d^{n}}{d\xi ^{n}}}{\widehat {f}}(\xi )\right\}=(-i2\pi x)^{n}f(x)}
[{\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}^{-1}\left\{{\frac {d^{n}}{d\xi ^{n}}}{\widehat {f}}(\xi
)\right\}=(-i2\pi x)^{n}f(x)}]⁠, so ⁠F { x n f ( x ) } = ( i 2 π ) n d n d ξ n f ^ ( ξ )
{\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}\left\{x^{n}f(x)\right\}=\left({\frac {i}{2\pi }}\right)^{n}{\frac
{d^{n}}{d\xi ^{n}}}{\widehat {f}}(\xi )} [{\displaystyle {\mathcal
{F}}\left\{x^{n}f(x)\right\}=\left({\frac {i}{2\pi }}\right)^{n}{\frac {d^{n}}{d\xi ^{n}}}{\widehat
{f}}(\xi )}]⁠.

By applying the Fourier transform and using these formulas, some [ordinary differential
equations][220] can be transformed into algebraic equations, which are much easier to solve. These
formulas also give rise to the [rule of thumb][221] "⁠ f ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)} [{\displaystyle
f(x)}]⁠ is smooth [if and only if][222] ⁠f ^ ( ξ ) {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi )}
[{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi )}]⁠ quickly falls to ⁠0 {\displaystyle 0} [{\displaystyle 0}]⁠ for
⁠| ξ | → ∞ {\displaystyle \vert \xi \vert \to \infty } [{\displaystyle \vert \xi \vert \to \infty
}]⁠". By using the analogous rules for the inverse Fourier transform, one can also say "⁠ f ( x )
{\displaystyle f(x)} [{\displaystyle f(x)}]⁠ quickly falls to ⁠0 {\displaystyle 0} [{\displaystyle 0}]⁠
for ⁠| x | → ∞ {\displaystyle \vert x\vert \to \infty } [{\displaystyle \vert x\vert \to \infty }]⁠ if
and only if ⁠f ^ ( ξ ) {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi )} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi )}]⁠ is
smooth."

### Eigenfunctions

See also: [Mehler kernel][223] and [Hermite polynomials § Hermite functions as eigenfunctions of the
Fourier transform][224]

The Fourier transform is a linear transform that has [eigenfunctions][225] obeying ⁠F [ ψ ] = λ ψ
{\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}[\psi ]=\lambda \psi } [{\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}[\psi ]=\lambda
\psi }]⁠, with ⁠λ ∈ C {\displaystyle \lambda \in \mathbb {C} } [{\displaystyle \lambda \in \mathbb {C}
}]⁠.

A set of eigenfunctions is found by noting that the homogeneous differential equation [ U ( 1 2 π d
d x ) + U ( x ) ] ψ ( x ) = 0 {\displaystyle \left[U\left({\frac {1}{2\pi }}{\frac
{d}{dx}}\right)+U(x)\right]\psi (x)=0} leads to eigenfunctions ψ ( x ) {\displaystyle \psi (x)}
[{\displaystyle \psi (x)}] of the Fourier transform F {\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}} [{\displaystyle
{\mathcal {F}}}] as long as the form of the equation remains invariant under Fourier
transform.^{[[note 6]][226]} In other words, every solution ψ ( x ) {\displaystyle \psi (x)}
[{\displaystyle \psi (x)}] and its Fourier transform ψ ^ ( ξ ) {\displaystyle {\widehat {\psi }}(\xi
)} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {\psi }}(\xi )}] obey the same equation. Assuming [uniqueness][227] of
the solutions, every solution ψ ( x ) {\displaystyle \psi (x)} [{\displaystyle \psi (x)}] must
therefore be an eigenfunction of the Fourier transform. The form of the equation remains unchanged
under Fourier transform if U ( x ) {\displaystyle U(x)} [{\displaystyle U(x)}] can be expanded in a
power series in which for all terms the same factor of either one of ⁠± 1 {\displaystyle \pm 1}
[{\displaystyle \pm 1}]⁠, ⁠± i {\displaystyle \pm i} [{\displaystyle \pm i}]⁠ arises from the factors i
n {\displaystyle i^{n}} [{\displaystyle i^{n}}] introduced by the [differentiation][228] rules upon
Fourier transforming the homogeneous differential equation because this factor may then be
cancelled. The simplest allowable U ( x ) = x {\displaystyle U(x)=x} [{\displaystyle U(x)=x}] leads
to the [standard normal distribution][229].^{[[22]][230]}

More generally, a set of eigenfunctions is also found by noting that the [differentiation][231]
rules imply that the [ordinary differential equation][232] [ W ( i 2 π d d x ) + W ( x ) ] ψ ( x ) =
C ψ ( x ) {\displaystyle \left[W\left({\frac {i}{2\pi }}{\frac {d}{dx}}\right)+W(x)\right]\psi
(x)=C\psi (x)} with C {\displaystyle C} [{\displaystyle C}] constant and W ( x ) {\displaystyle
W(x)} [{\displaystyle W(x)}] being a non-constant even function remains invariant in form when
applying the Fourier transform F {\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}} [{\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}}] to
both sides of the equation. The simplest example is provided by ⁠W ( x ) = x 2 {\displaystyle
W(x)=x^{2}} [{\displaystyle W(x)=x^{2}}]⁠, which is equivalent to considering the Schrödinger
equation for the [quantum harmonic oscillator][233].^{[[23]][234]} The corresponding solutions
provide an important choice of an orthonormal basis for [L²(**R**)][235] and are given by the
"physicist's" [Hermite functions][236]. Equivalently one may use ψ n ( x ) = 2 4 n ! e − π x 2 H e n
( 2 x π ) , {\displaystyle \psi _{n}(x)={\frac {\sqrt[{4}]{2}}{\sqrt {n!}}}e^{-\pi x^{2}}\mathrm
{He} _{n}\left(2x{\sqrt {\pi }}\right),} where ⁠H e n ( x ) {\displaystyle \mathrm {He} _{n}(x)}
[{\displaystyle \mathrm {He} _{n}(x)}]⁠ are the "probabilist's" [Hermite polynomials][237], defined
as H e n ( x ) = ( − 1 ) n e 1 2 x 2 ( d d x ) n e − 1 2 x 2 . {\displaystyle \mathrm {He}
_{n}(x)=(-1)^{n}e^{{\frac {1}{2}}x^{2}}\left({\frac {d}{dx}}\right)^{n}e^{-{\frac {1}{2}}x^{2}}.}

Under this convention for the Fourier transform, we have that ψ ^ n ( ξ ) = ( − i ) n ψ n ( ξ ) .
{\displaystyle {\widehat {\psi }}_{n}(\xi )=(-i)^{n}\psi _{n}(\xi ).}

In other words, the Hermite functions form a complete [orthonormal][238] system of
[eigenfunctions][239] for the Fourier transform on ⁠L 2 ( R ) {\displaystyle L^{2}(\mathbb {R} )}
[{\displaystyle L^{2}(\mathbb {R} )}]⁠.^{[[15]][240]}^{[[24]][241]} However, this choice of
eigenfunctions is not unique. Because of F 4 = i d {\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}^{4}=\mathrm {id} }
[{\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}^{4}=\mathrm {id} }] there are only four different [eigenvalues][242]
of the Fourier transform (the fourth roots of unity ⁠± 1 {\displaystyle \pm 1} [{\displaystyle \pm
1}]⁠ and ⁠± i {\displaystyle \pm i} [{\displaystyle \pm i}]⁠) and any linear combination of
eigenfunctions with the same eigenvalue gives another eigenfunction.^{[[25]][243]} As a consequence
of this, it is possible to decompose L²(**R**) as a direct sum of four spaces H0, H1, H2, and H3
where the Fourier transform acts on Hk simply by multiplication by i^{k}.

Since the complete set of Hermite functions ψn provides a resolution of the identity they
diagonalize the Fourier operator, i.e. the Fourier transform can be represented by such a sum of
terms weighted by the above eigenvalues, and these sums can be explicitly summed: F [ f ] ( ξ ) = ∫
d x f ( x ) ∑ n ≥ 0 ( − i ) n ψ n ( x ) ψ n ( ξ )   . {\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}[f](\xi )=\int
dxf(x)\sum _{n\geq 0}(-i)^{n}\psi _{n}(x)\psi _{n}(\xi )~.}

This approach to define the Fourier transform was first proposed by [Norbert
Wiener][244].^{[[26]][245]} Among other properties, Hermite functions decrease exponentially fast in
both frequency and time domains, and they are thus used to define a generalization of the Fourier
transform, namely the [fractional Fourier transform][246] used in time–frequency
analysis.^{[[27]][247]} In [physics][248], this transform was introduced by [Edward
Condon][249].^{[[28]][250]} This [change of basis][251] becomes possible because the Fourier
transform is a unitary transform when using the right [conventions][252]. Consequently, under the
proper conditions it may be expected to result from a self-adjoint generator N {\displaystyle N}
[{\displaystyle N}] via^{[[29]][253]} F [ ψ ] = e − i t N ψ . {\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}[\psi
]=e^{-itN}\psi .}

The operator N {\displaystyle N} [{\displaystyle N}] is the [number operator][254] of the quantum
harmonic oscillator written as^{[[30]][255]}^{[[31]][256]} N ≡ 1 4 π ( 2 π x − ∂ ∂ x ) ( 2 π x + ∂ ∂
x ) = − 1 4 π ∂ 2 ∂ x 2 + π x 2 − 1 2 . {\displaystyle N\equiv {\frac {1}{4\pi }}\left(2\pi x-{\frac
{\partial }{\partial x}}\right)\left(2\pi x+{\frac {\partial }{\partial x}}\right)=-{\frac {1}{4\pi
}}{\frac {\partial ^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}}+\pi x^{2}-{\frac {1}{2}}.}

It can be interpreted as the [generator][257] of [fractional Fourier transforms][258] for arbitrary
values of t, and of the conventional continuous Fourier transform F {\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}}
[{\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}}] for the particular value ⁠t = π / 2 {\displaystyle t=\pi /2}
[{\displaystyle t=\pi /2}]⁠, with the [Mehler kernel][259] implementing the corresponding [active
transform][260]. The eigenfunctions of N {\displaystyle N} [{\displaystyle N}] are the [Hermite
functions][261] ⁠ψ n ( x ) {\displaystyle \psi _{n}(x)} [{\displaystyle \psi _{n}(x)}]⁠, which are
therefore also eigenfunctions of ⁠F {\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}} [{\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}}]⁠.

Upon extending the Fourier transform to [distributions][262] the [Dirac comb][263] is also an
eigenfunction of the Fourier transform.

### Inversion and periodicity

Further information: [Fourier inversion theorem][264] and [Fractional Fourier transform][265]

Under suitable conditions on the function ⁠f {\displaystyle f} [{\displaystyle f}]⁠, it can be
recovered from its Fourier transform ⁠f ^ {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}} [{\displaystyle {\widehat
{f}}}]⁠. Indeed, denoting the Fourier transform operator by ⁠F {\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}}
[{\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}}]⁠, so ⁠F f := f ^ {\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}f:={\widehat {f}}}
[{\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}f:={\widehat {f}}}]⁠, then for suitable functions, applying the Fourier
transform twice simply flips the function: ⁠( F 2 f ) ( x ) = f ( − x ) {\displaystyle
\left({\mathcal {F}}^{2}f\right)(x)=f(-x)} [{\displaystyle \left({\mathcal
{F}}^{2}f\right)(x)=f(-x)}]⁠, which can be interpreted as "reversing time". Since reversing time is
two-periodic, applying this twice yields ⁠F 4 ( f ) = f {\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}^{4}(f)=f}
[{\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}^{4}(f)=f}]⁠, so the Fourier transform operator is four-periodic, and
similarly the inverse Fourier transform can be obtained by applying the Fourier transform three
times: ⁠F 3 ( f ^ ) = f {\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}^{3}\left({\widehat {f}}\right)=f}
[{\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}^{3}\left({\widehat {f}}\right)=f}]⁠. In particular the Fourier
transform is invertible (under suitable conditions).

More precisely, defining the parity operator P {\displaystyle {\mathcal {P}}} [{\displaystyle
{\mathcal {P}}}] such that ⁠( P f ) ( x ) = f ( − x ) {\displaystyle ({\mathcal {P}}f)(x)=f(-x)}
[{\displaystyle ({\mathcal {P}}f)(x)=f(-x)}]⁠, we have: F 0 = i d , F 1 = F , F 2 = P , F 3 = F − 1 =
P ∘ F = F ∘ P , F 4 = i d {\displaystyle {\begin{aligned}{\mathcal {F}}^{0}&=\mathrm {id}
,\\{\mathcal {F}}^{1}&={\mathcal {F}},\\{\mathcal {F}}^{2}&={\mathcal {P}},\\{\mathcal
{F}}^{3}&={\mathcal {F}}^{-1}={\mathcal {P}}\circ {\mathcal {F}}={\mathcal {F}}\circ {\mathcal
{P}},\\{\mathcal {F}}^{4}&=\mathrm {id} \end{aligned}}} These equalities of operators require
careful definition of the space of functions in question, defining equality of functions (equality
at every point? equality [almost everywhere][266]?) and defining equality of operators – that is,
defining the topology on the function space and operator space in question. These are not true for
all functions, but are true under various conditions, which are the content of the various forms of
the [Fourier inversion theorem][267].

This fourfold periodicity of the Fourier transform is similar to a rotation of the plane by 90°,
particularly as the two-fold iteration yields a reversal, and in fact this analogy can be made
precise. While the Fourier transform can simply be interpreted as switching the time domain and the
frequency domain, with the inverse Fourier transform switching them back, more geometrically it can
be interpreted as a rotation by 90° in the [time–frequency domain][268] (considering time as the ⁠x
{\displaystyle x} [{\displaystyle x}]⁠-axis and frequency as the ⁠y {\displaystyle y} [{\displaystyle
y}]⁠-axis), and the Fourier transform can be generalized to the [fractional Fourier transform][269],
which involves rotations by other angles. This can be further generalized to [linear canonical
transformations][270], which can be visualized as the action of the [special linear group][271]
[SL2(**R**)][272] on the time–frequency plane, with the preserved symplectic form corresponding to
the [uncertainty principle][273], below. This approach is particularly studied in [signal
processing][274], under [time–frequency analysis][275].

### Connection with the Heisenberg group

The [Heisenberg group][276] is a certain [group][277] of [unitary operators][278] on the [Hilbert
space][279] L²(**R**) of square integrable complex valued functions f on the real line, generated by
the translations (Ty f)(x) = f (x + y) and multiplication by e^{i2πξx}, (Mξ f)(x) = e^{i2πξx} f (x).
These operators do not commute, as their (group) commutator is ( M ξ − 1 T y − 1 M ξ T y f ) ( x ) =
e i 2 π ξ y f ( x ) , {\displaystyle \left(M_{\xi }^{-1}T_{y}^{-1}M_{\xi }T_{y}f\right)(x)=e^{i2\pi
\xi y}f(x),} which is multiplication by the constant (independent of x) e^{i2πξy} ∈ U(1) (the
[circle group][280] of unit modulus complex numbers). As an abstract group, the Heisenberg group is
the three-dimensional [Lie group][281] of triples (x, ξ, z) ∈ **R**² × U(1), with the group law ( x
1 , ξ 1 , t 1 ) ⋅ ( x 2 , ξ 2 , t 2 ) = ( x 1 + x 2 , ξ 1 + ξ 2 , t 1 t 2 e − 2 i π x 1 ξ 2 ) .
{\displaystyle \left(x_{1},\xi _{1},t_{1}\right)\cdot \left(x_{2},\xi
_{2},t_{2}\right)=\left(x_{1}+x_{2},\xi _{1}+\xi _{2},t_{1}t_{2}e^{-2i\pi x_{1}\xi _{2}}\right).}

Denote the Heisenberg group by H1. The above procedure describes not only the group structure, but
also a standard [unitary representation][282] of H1 on a Hilbert space, which we denote by ρ : H1 →
B(L²(**R**)). Define the linear automorphism of **R**² by J ( x ξ ) = ( − ξ x ) {\displaystyle
J{\begin{pmatrix}x\\\xi \end{pmatrix}}={\begin{pmatrix}-\xi \\x\end{pmatrix}}} so that J² = −I. This
J can be extended to a unique automorphism of H1: j ( x , ξ , t ) = ( − ξ , x , t e − i 2 π ξ x ) .
{\displaystyle j\left(x,\xi ,t\right)=\left(-\xi ,x,te^{-i2\pi \xi x}\right).}

According to the [Stone–von Neumann theorem][283], the unitary representations ρ and ρ ∘ j are
unitarily equivalent, so there is a unique intertwiner W ∈ U(L²(**R**)) such that ρ ∘ j = W ρ W ∗ .
{\displaystyle \rho \circ j=W\rho W^{*}.} This operator W is the Fourier transform.

Many of the standard properties of the Fourier transform are immediate consequences of this more
general framework.^{[[32]][284]} For example, the square of the Fourier transform, W², is an
intertwiner associated with J² = −I, and so we have (W²f)(x) = f (−x) is the reflection of the
original function f.

## Complex domain

The [integral][285] for the Fourier transform f ^ ( ξ ) = ∫ − ∞ ∞ e − 2 i π t ξ f ( t ) d t
{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi )=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }e^{-2i\pi t\xi }f(t)\,dt} can be
studied for [complex][286] values of its argument ξ. Depending on the properties of f, this might
not converge off the real axis at all, or it might converge to a [complex][287] [analytic
function][288] for all values of ξ = σ + iτ, or something in between.^{[[33]][289]}

The [Paley–Wiener theorem][290] says that f is smooth (i.e., n-times differentiable for all positive
integers n) and compactly supported if and only if f̂ (σ + iτ) is a [holomorphic function][291] for
which there exists a [constant][292] a > 0 such that for any [integer][293] n ≥ 0, | ξ n f ^ ( ξ ) |
≤ C n e 2 π a | τ | {\displaystyle \left\vert \xi ^{n}{\widehat {f}}(\xi )\right\vert \leq
C_{n}e^{2\pi a\vert \tau \vert }} for some constant C_n. (In this case, f is supported on [−a, a].)
This can be expressed by saying that f̂ is an [entire function][294] that is [rapidly
decreasing][295] in σ (for fixed τ) and of exponential growth in τ (uniformly in σ).^{[[34]][296]}

(If f is not smooth, but only L², a corresponding version holds with the rapid-decrease condition
replaced by the appropriate L² condition.^{[[35]][297]}) The space of such functions of a [complex
variable][298] is called the Paley–Wiener space. This theorem has been generalised to semisimple
[Lie groups][299].^{[[36]][300]}

If f is supported on the half-line t ≥ 0, then f is said to be "causal" because the [impulse
response function][301] of a physically realisable [filter][302] must have this property, as no
effect can precede its cause. [Paley][303] and Wiener showed that then, under suitable integrability
hypotheses, f̂ extends to a [holomorphic function][304] on the complex lower half-plane τ < 0 that
tends to zero as τ goes to −∞.^{[[37]][305]} A simple converse in this form is false; precise
converses require additional growth or Hardy-space hypotheses.^{[[38]][306]}

### Laplace transform

See also: [Laplace transform § Fourier transform][307]

The Fourier transform f̂(ξ) is related to the [Laplace transform][308] F(s), which is also used for
the solution of [differential equations][309] and the analysis of [filters][310].

It may happen that a function f for which the Fourier integral does not converge on the real axis at
all nevertheless has a complex Fourier transform defined in some region of the [complex plane][311].

For example, if f is causal and of exponential growth, i.e., f ( t ) = 0 ( t < 0 ) , | f ( t ) | < C
e a t ( t ≥ 0 ) {\displaystyle f(t)=0\quad (t<0),\qquad \vert f(t)\vert <Ce^{at}\quad (t\geq 0)} for
some constants C, a ≥ 0, then^{[[39]][312]} f ^ ( i τ ) = ∫ 0 ∞ e 2 π τ t f ( t ) d t ,
{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(i\tau )=\int _{0}^{\infty }e^{2\pi \tau t}f(t)\,dt,} convergent for
all 2πτ < −a, is a one-sided Laplace transform of f.

The usual one-sided version of the Laplace transform is F ( s ) = ∫ 0 ∞ f ( t ) e − s t d t .
{\displaystyle F(s)=\int _{0}^{\infty }f(t)e^{-st}\,dt.}

If f is causal and the integrals converge, then ⁠f ^ ( i τ ) = F ( − 2 π τ ) {\displaystyle {\widehat
{f}}(i\tau )=F(-2\pi \tau )} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(i\tau )=F(-2\pi \tau )}]⁠. Thus,
extending the Fourier transform to the complex domain means it includes the Laplace transform as a
special case in the case of causal functions—but with the change of variable s = i2πξ.

From another, perhaps more classical viewpoint, the Laplace transform by its form involves an
additional exponential regulating term that lets it converge outside of the imaginary line where the
Fourier transform is defined. As such it can converge for functions and integrals with at most
exponential growth in the regulated direction, whereas the original Fourier decomposition cannot,
enabling analysis of systems with divergent or critical elements. Two particular examples from
linear signal processing are the construction of allpass filter networks from critical comb and
mitigating filters via exact pole-zero cancellation on the unit circle. Such designs are common in
audio processing, where highly nonlinear [phase response][313] is sought for, as in reverb.

Furthermore, when extended pulselike impulse responses are sought for signal processing work, the
easiest way to produce them is to have one circuit that produces a divergent time response, and then
to cancel its divergence through a delayed opposite and compensatory response. There, only the delay
circuit in-between admits a classical Fourier description, which is critical. Both the circuits to
the side are unstable, and do not admit a convergent Fourier decomposition. However, they do admit a
Laplace domain description, with compatible half-planes of convergence in the complex plane (or in
the discrete case, the Z-plane), wherein their effects cancel.

In modern mathematics the Laplace transform is conventionally subsumed under the aegis of Fourier
methods. Both of them are subsumed by the far more general, and more abstract, idea of [harmonic
analysis][314].

### Inversion

Still with ⁠ξ = σ + i τ {\displaystyle \xi =\sigma +i\tau } [{\displaystyle \xi =\sigma +i\tau }]⁠, if
f ^ {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}}] is complex analytic for a ≤ τ ≤
b and has sufficient decay in horizontal strips, then ∫ − ∞ ∞ f ^ ( σ + i a ) e i 2 π ( σ + i a ) t
d σ = ∫ − ∞ ∞ f ^ ( σ + i b ) e i 2 π ( σ + i b ) t d σ {\displaystyle \int _{-\infty }^{\infty
}{\widehat {f}}(\sigma +ia)e^{i2\pi (\sigma +ia)t}\,d\sigma =\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }{\widehat
{f}}(\sigma +ib)e^{i2\pi (\sigma +ib)t}\,d\sigma } by [Cauchy's integral theorem][315]. Therefore,
the Fourier inversion formula can use integration along different lines, parallel to the real
axis.^{[[40]][316]}

Theorem: If f(t) = 0 for t < 0, and |f(t)| < Ce^{at} for some constants C, a > 0 and t ≥ 0, then f (
t ) = ∫ − ∞ ∞ f ^ ( σ + i τ ) e i 2 π ( σ + i τ ) t d σ , {\displaystyle f(t)=\int _{-\infty
}^{\infty }{\widehat {f}}(\sigma +i\tau )e^{i2\pi (\sigma +i\tau )t}\,d\sigma ,} for any τ < −⁠a/2π⁠,
under the usual hypotheses for Fourier inversion.

This theorem implies the [Mellin inversion formula][317] for the Laplace
transformation,^{[[39]][318]} f ( t ) = 1 i 2 π ∫ b − i ∞ b + i ∞ F ( s ) e s t d s {\displaystyle
f(t)={\frac {1}{i2\pi }}\int _{b-i\infty }^{b+i\infty }F(s)e^{st}\,ds} for any b > a, where F(s) is
the Laplace transform of f(t).

The hypotheses can be weakened, as in standard Fourier inversion results, to f(t) e^{−at} being L¹,
provided that f be of [bounded variation][319] in a closed neighborhood of t (cf. [Dini test][320]),
the value of f at t be taken to be the [arithmetic mean][321] of the left and right limits, and that
the integrals be taken in the sense of Cauchy principal values.^{[[41]][322]}

L² versions of these inversion formulas are also available.^{[[42]][323]}

## Fourier transform on Euclidean space

The Fourier transform can be defined in any arbitrary number of dimensions n. As with the
one-dimensional case, there are many conventions. For an integrable function f(**x**), this article
takes the definition: f ^ ( ξ ) = F ( f ) ( ξ ) = ∫ R n f ( x ) e − i 2 π ξ ⋅ x d x {\displaystyle
{\widehat {f}}({\boldsymbol {\xi }})={\mathcal {F}}(f)({\boldsymbol {\xi }})=\int _{\mathbb {R}
^{n}}f(\mathbf {x} )e^{-i2\pi {\boldsymbol {\xi }}\cdot \mathbf {x} }\,d\mathbf {x} } where **x**
and **ξ** are n-dimensional [vectors][324], and **x** · **ξ** is the [dot product][325] of the
vectors. Alternatively, **ξ** can be viewed as belonging to the [dual vector space][326] ⁠R n ⋆
{\displaystyle \mathbb {R} ^{n\star }} [{\displaystyle \mathbb {R} ^{n\star }}]⁠, in which case the
dot product becomes the [contraction][327] of **x** and **ξ**, usually written as ⟨**x**, **ξ**⟩.

All of the basic properties listed above hold for the n-dimensional Fourier transform, as do
Plancherel's and Parseval's theorem. When the function is integrable, the Fourier transform is still
uniformly continuous and the [Riemann–Lebesgue lemma][328] holds.^{[[19]][329]}

### Uncertainty principle

Further information: [Uncertainty principle][330]

Generally speaking, the more concentrated f(x) is, the more spread out its Fourier transform f̂(ξ)
must be. In particular, the scaling property of the Fourier transform may be seen as saying: if we
squeeze a function in x, its Fourier transform stretches out in ξ. It is not possible to arbitrarily
concentrate both a function and its Fourier transform.

The trade-off between the compaction of a function and its Fourier transform can be formalized in
the form of an [uncertainty principle][331] by viewing a function and its Fourier transform as
[conjugate variables][332] with respect to the [symplectic form][333] on the [time–frequency
domain][334]: from the point of view of the [linear canonical transformation][335], the Fourier
transform is rotation by 90° in the time–frequency domain, and preserves the symplectic form.

Suppose f(x) is an integrable and [square-integrable][336] function. [Without loss of
generality][337], assume that f(x) is normalized: ∫ − ∞ ∞ | f ( x ) | 2 d x = 1. {\displaystyle \int
_{-\infty }^{\infty }|f(x)|^{2}\,dx=1.}

It follows from the [Plancherel theorem][338] that f̂(ξ) is also normalized.

The spread around x = 0 may be measured by the dispersion about zero defined by^{[[43]][339]} D 0 (
f ) = ∫ − ∞ ∞ x 2 | f ( x ) | 2 d x . {\displaystyle D_{0}(f)=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty
}x^{2}|f(x)|^{2}\,dx.}

In probability terms, this is the [second moment][340] of |f(x)|² about zero.

The uncertainty principle states that, if f(x) is absolutely continuous and the functions x·f(x) and
f′(x) are square integrable, then D 0 ( f ) D 0 ( f ^ ) ≥ 1 16 π 2 . {\displaystyle
D_{0}(f)D_{0}({\widehat {f}})\geq {\frac {1}{16\pi ^{2}}}.}

The equality is attained only in the case f ( x ) = C 1 e − π x 2 σ 2 ∴ f ^ ( ξ ) = σ C 1 e − π σ 2
ξ 2 {\displaystyle {\begin{aligned}f(x)&=C_{1}\,e^{-\pi {\frac {x^{2}}{\sigma ^{2}}}}\\\therefore
{\widehat {f}}(\xi )&=\sigma C_{1}\,e^{-\pi \sigma ^{2}\xi ^{2}}\end{aligned}}} where σ > 0 is
arbitrary and C1 = ⁠⁴√2/√σ⁠ so that f is L²-normalized. In other words, where f is a (normalized)
[Gaussian function][341] with variance σ²/2π, centered at zero, and its Fourier transform is a
Gaussian function with variance σ^{−2}/2π. Gaussian functions are examples of [Schwartz
functions][342] (see the discussion on tempered distributions below).

In fact, this inequality implies that: ( ∫ − ∞ ∞ ( x − x 0 ) 2 | f ( x ) | 2 d x ) ( ∫ − ∞ ∞ ( ξ − ξ
0 ) 2 | f ^ ( ξ ) | 2 d ξ ) ≥ 1 16 π 2 , ∀ x 0 , ξ 0 ∈ R . {\displaystyle \left(\int _{-\infty
}^{\infty }(x-x_{0})^{2}|f(x)|^{2}\,dx\right)\left(\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }(\xi -\xi
_{0})^{2}\left|{\widehat {f}}(\xi )\right|^{2}\,d\xi \right)\geq {\frac {1}{16\pi ^{2}}},\quad
\forall x_{0},\xi _{0}\in \mathbb {R} .} In [quantum mechanics][343], the [momentum][344] and
position [wave functions][345] are Fourier transform pairs, up to a factor of the [Planck
constant][346]. With this constant properly taken into account, the inequality above becomes the
statement of the [Heisenberg uncertainty principle][347].^{[[44]][348]}

A stronger uncertainty principle is the [Hirschman uncertainty principle][349], which is expressed
as: H ( | f | 2 ) + H ( | f ^ | 2 ) ≥ log ⁡( e 2 ) {\displaystyle
H\left(\left|f\right|^{2}\right)+H\left(\left|{\widehat {f}}\right|^{2}\right)\geq \log \left({\frac
{e}{2}}\right)} where H(p) is the [differential entropy][350] of the [probability density
function][351] p(x): H ( p ) = − ∫ − ∞ ∞ p ( x ) log ⁡( p ( x ) ) d x {\displaystyle H(p)=-\int
_{-\infty }^{\infty }p(x)\log {\bigl (}p(x){\bigr )}\,dx} where the logarithms may be in any base
that is consistent. The equality is attained for a Gaussian, as in the previous case.

### Sine and cosine transforms

Main article: [Sine and cosine transforms][352]

Fourier's original formulation of the transform did not use complex numbers, but rather sines and
cosines. Statisticians and others still use this form. An absolutely integrable function f for which
Fourier inversion holds can be expanded in terms of genuine frequencies (avoiding negative
frequencies, which are sometimes considered hard to interpret physically^{[[45]][353]}) λ by f ( t )
= ∫ 0 ∞ ( a ( λ ) cos ⁡( 2 π λ t ) + b ( λ ) sin ⁡( 2 π λ t ) ) d λ . {\displaystyle f(t)=\int
_{0}^{\infty }{\bigl (}a(\lambda )\cos(2\pi \lambda t)+b(\lambda )\sin(2\pi \lambda t){\bigr
)}\,d\lambda .}

This is called an expansion as a [trigonometric integral][354], or a Fourier integral expansion. The
coefficient functions a and b can be found by using variants of the Fourier cosine transform and the
Fourier sine transform (the normalisations are, again, not standardised): a ( λ ) = 2 ∫ − ∞ ∞ f ( t
) cos ⁡( 2 π λ t ) d t {\displaystyle a(\lambda )=2\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }f(t)\cos(2\pi \lambda
t)\,dt} and b ( λ ) = 2 ∫ − ∞ ∞ f ( t ) sin ⁡( 2 π λ t ) d t . {\displaystyle b(\lambda )=2\int
_{-\infty }^{\infty }f(t)\sin(2\pi \lambda t)\,dt.}

Older literature refers to the two transform functions, the Fourier cosine transform, a, and the
Fourier sine transform, b.

The function f can be recovered from the sine and cosine transform using f ( t ) = 2 ∫ 0 ∞ ∫ − ∞ ∞ f
( τ ) cos ⁡( 2 π λ ( τ − t ) ) d τ d λ . {\displaystyle f(t)=2\int _{0}^{\infty }\int _{-\infty
}^{\infty }f(\tau )\cos {\bigl (}2\pi \lambda (\tau -t){\bigr )}\,d\tau \,d\lambda .} together with
trigonometric identities. This is referred to as Fourier's integral
formula.^{[[39]][355]}^{[[46]][356]}^{[[47]][357]}^{[[48]][358]}

### Spherical harmonics

Let the set of [homogeneous][359] [harmonic][360] [polynomials][361] of degree k on **R**^{n} be
denoted by **A**k. The set **A**k consists of the [solid spherical harmonics][362] of degree k. The
solid spherical harmonics play a similar role in higher dimensions to the Hermite polynomials in
dimension one. Specifically, if f(x) = e^{−π|x|²}P(x) for some P(x) in **A**k, then ⁠f ^ ( ξ ) = i −
k f ( ξ ) {\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi )=i^{-k}f(\xi )} [{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi
)=i^{-k}f(\xi )}]⁠. Let the set **H**k be the closure in L²(**R**^{n}) of linear combinations of
functions of the form f(|x|)P(x) where P(x) is in **A**k. The space L²(**R**^{n}) is then a direct
sum of the spaces **H**k and the Fourier transform maps each space **H**k to itself and it is
possible to characterize the action of the Fourier transform on each space **H**k.^{[[19]][363]}

Let f(x) = f0(|x|)P(x) (with P(x) in **A**k), then f ^ ( ξ ) = F 0 ( | ξ | ) P ( ξ ) {\displaystyle
{\widehat {f}}(\xi )=F_{0}(|\xi |)P(\xi )} where F 0 ( r ) = 2 π i − k r − n + 2 k − 2 2 ∫ 0 ∞ f 0 (
s ) J n + 2 k − 2 2 ( 2 π r s ) s n + 2 k 2 d s . {\displaystyle F_{0}(r)=2\pi i^{-k}r^{-{\frac
{n+2k-2}{2}}}\int _{0}^{\infty }f_{0}(s)J_{\frac {n+2k-2}{2}}(2\pi rs)s^{\frac {n+2k}{2}}\,ds.}

Here J(n + 2k − 2)/2 denotes the [Bessel function][364] of the first kind with order ⁠n + 2k − 2/2⁠.
When k = 0 this gives a useful formula for the Fourier transform of a radial function.^{[[49]][365]}
This is essentially the [Hankel transform][366]. Moreover, there is a simple recursion relating the
cases n + 2 and n^{[[50]][367]} allowing to compute, e.g., the three-dimensional Fourier transform
of a radial function from the one-dimensional one.

### Restriction problems

See also: [Fourier extension operator][368]

In higher dimensions it becomes interesting to study restriction problems for the Fourier transform.
The Fourier transform of an integrable function is continuous and the restriction of this function
to any set is defined. But for a square-integrable function the Fourier transform could be a general
class of square integrable functions. As such, the restriction of the Fourier transform of an
L²(**R**^{n}) function cannot be defined on sets of measure 0. It is still an active area of study
to understand restriction problems in L^{p} for 1 < p < 2. It is possible in some cases to define
the restriction of a Fourier transform to a set S, provided S has non-zero curvature. The case when
S is the unit sphere in **R**^{n} is of particular interest. In this case the Tomas–[Stein][369]
restriction theorem states that the restriction of the Fourier transform to the unit sphere in
**R**^{n} is a bounded operator on L^{p} provided 1 ≤ p ≤ ⁠2n + 2/n + 3⁠.

One notable difference between the Fourier transform in 1 dimension versus higher dimensions
concerns the partial sum operator. Consider an increasing collection of measurable sets ER indexed
by R ∈ (0, ∞): such as balls of radius R centered at the origin, or cubes of side 2R. For a given
integrable function f, consider the function fR defined by: f R ( x ) = ∫ E R f ^ ( ξ ) e i 2 π x ⋅
ξ d ξ , x ∈ R n . {\displaystyle f_{R}(x)=\int _{E_{R}}{\widehat {f}}(\xi )e^{i2\pi x\cdot \xi
}\,d\xi ,\quad x\in \mathbb {R} ^{n}.}

Suppose in addition that f ∈ L^{p}(**R**^{n}). For n = 1 and 1 < p < ∞, if one takes ER = (−R, R),
then fR converges to f in L^{p} as R tends to infinity, by the boundedness of the [Hilbert
transform][370]. Naively one may hope the same holds true for n > 1. In the case that ER is taken to
be a cube with side length R, then convergence still holds. Another natural candidate is the
Euclidean ball ER = {ξ : |ξ| < R}. In order for this partial sum operator to converge, it is
necessary that the [multiplier][371] for the unit ball be bounded in L^{p}(**R**^{n}). For n ≥ 2 it
is a celebrated theorem of [Charles Fefferman][372] that the multiplier for the unit ball is never
bounded unless p = 2.^{[[51]][373]} In fact, when p ≠ 2, this shows that not only may fR fail to
converge to f in L^{p}, but for some functions f ∈ L^{p}(**R**^{n}), fR is not even an element of
L^{p}.

## Fourier transform on function spaces

See also: [Riesz–Thorin theorem][374]

The definition of the Fourier transform naturally extends from L 1 ( R ) {\displaystyle
L^{1}(\mathbb {R} )} [{\displaystyle L^{1}(\mathbb {R} )}] to ⁠L 1 ( R n ) {\displaystyle
L^{1}(\mathbb {R} ^{n})} [{\displaystyle L^{1}(\mathbb {R} ^{n})}]⁠. That is, if f ∈ L 1 ( R n )
{\displaystyle f\in L^{1}(\mathbb {R} ^{n})} [{\displaystyle f\in L^{1}(\mathbb {R} ^{n})}] then the
Fourier transform F : L 1 ( R n ) → L ∞ ( R n ) {\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}:L^{1}(\mathbb {R}
^{n})\to L^{\infty }(\mathbb {R} ^{n})} [{\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}:L^{1}(\mathbb {R} ^{n})\to
L^{\infty }(\mathbb {R} ^{n})}] is given by f ( x ) ↦ f ^ ( ξ ) = ∫ R n f ( x ) e − i 2 π ξ ⋅ x d x
, ∀ ξ ∈ R n . {\displaystyle f(x)\mapsto {\widehat {f}}(\xi )=\int _{\mathbb {R} ^{n}}f(x)e^{-i2\pi
\xi \cdot x}\,dx,\quad \forall \xi \in \mathbb {R} ^{n}.} This operator is [bounded][375] as sup ξ ∈
R n | f ^ ( ξ ) | ≤ ∫ R n | f ( x ) | d x , {\displaystyle \sup _{\xi \in \mathbb {R}
^{n}}\left\vert {\widehat {f}}(\xi )\right\vert \leq \int _{\mathbb {R} ^{n}}\vert f(x)\vert \,dx,}
which shows that its [operator norm][376] is bounded by 1. The [Riemann–Lebesgue lemma][377] shows
that if f ∈ L 1 ( R n ) {\displaystyle f\in L^{1}(\mathbb {R} ^{n})} [{\displaystyle f\in
L^{1}(\mathbb {R} ^{n})}] then its Fourier transform actually belongs to the [space of continuous
functions that vanish at infinity][378], i.e., ⁠f ^ ∈ C 0 ( R n ) ⊂ L ∞ ( R n ) {\displaystyle
{\widehat {f}}\in C_{0}(\mathbb {R} ^{n})\subset L^{\infty }(\mathbb {R} ^{n})} [{\displaystyle
{\widehat {f}}\in C_{0}(\mathbb {R} ^{n})\subset L^{\infty }(\mathbb {R}
^{n})}]⁠.^{[[52]][379]}^{[[53]][380]} Furthermore, the [image][381] of L 1 {\displaystyle L^{1}}
[{\displaystyle L^{1}}] under F {\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}} [{\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}}] is a
strict subset of ⁠C 0 ( R n ) {\displaystyle C_{0}(\mathbb {R} ^{n})} [{\displaystyle C_{0}(\mathbb
{R} ^{n})}]⁠.^{[[54]][382]}

Similarly to the case of one variable, the Fourier transform can be defined on ⁠L 2 ( R n )
{\displaystyle L^{2}(\mathbb {R} ^{n})} [{\displaystyle L^{2}(\mathbb {R} ^{n})}]⁠. The Fourier
transform in L 2 ( R n ) {\displaystyle L^{2}(\mathbb {R} ^{n})} [{\displaystyle L^{2}(\mathbb {R}
^{n})}] is no longer given by an ordinary Lebesgue integral, although it can be computed by an
[improper integral][383], i.e., f ^ ( ξ ) = lim R → ∞ ∫ | x | ≤ R f ( x ) e − i 2 π ξ ⋅ x d x
{\displaystyle {\widehat {f}}(\xi )=\lim _{R\to \infty }\int _{|x|\leq R}f(x)e^{-i2\pi \xi \cdot
x}\,dx} where the limit is taken in the L² sense.^{[[note 7]][384]}^{[[55]][385]}

Furthermore, F : L 2 ( R n ) → L 2 ( R n ) {\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}:L^{2}(\mathbb {R} ^{n})\to
L^{2}(\mathbb {R} ^{n})} [{\displaystyle {\mathcal {F}}:L^{2}(\mathbb {R} ^{n})\to L^{2}(\mathbb {R}
